assistance of counsel is based, that the ap-
pellant show that the specific act or omis-
sion was a substantial and serious deficien-
cy measurably below the standard expected
of competent attorneys, that the appellant
show that the deficiency, under the circum-
stances, was so substantial as to have prob-
ably affected the outcome of the proceed-
ings, and that if the above three factors are
established, the state still has the opportu-
nity to rebut the claim of ineffective assist-
ance by showing beyond a reasonable doubt
that there was in fact no prejudice. U.S.
C.A. Const.Amend. 6.

2. Criminal Law <=641.13(7)

Right of the accused to reasonably
competent assistance of legal counsel does
not entitle him to have every conceivable

constitutional challenge pressed upon the
court. U.S.C.A. Const.Amend. 6.

3. Criminal Law <641.13(2)

It was within the range of competency
expected of attorneys in criminal cases for
defense counsel to act as he did regarding
allegedly discriminatory selection of grand
jurors, and thus his action did not constitute
ineffective assistance of counsel. U.S.C.A.
Const.Amend. 6.

4. Criminal Law <=641.13(6)

Where defense counsel did in fact
present witnesses who testified concerning
petitioner’s character and background and
petitioner did not identify any one of the
enumerated items of fact which he relied on
as a specific omission which amounted to a
substantial deficiency and which under the
circumstances probably affected the out-
come of the trial, he was not denied effee-
tive assistance of counsel on ground that
counsel failed to gather and present evi-
dence of mitigating circumstances. U.S.
C.A. Const.Amend 6.

5. Criminal Law ¢=641.13(2)

Defense counsel was not deficient in
failing to object to jury instruction on proc-
ess of weighing aggravating and mitigating
circumstances and failing to request an al-
ternative instruction, in that instruction
given was proper.

423 SOUTHERN REPORTER, 2d SERIES

6. Criminal Law <=641.13(7)

Appellate counsel was not ineffective
due to fact that he failed to raise as an
issue the trial court’s refusal to give jury
instruction requested by defense counsel
relative to the aggravating circumstance
that murder was heinous, atrocious or cruel,
in that defense counsel did not object to
instruction concerning elements of this ag-
gravating circumstance which was given by
trial court so question of whether this in-
struction was indeed erroneous could not
properly have been raised on appeal and
petitioner had failed to demonstrate that
omission to raise issue on appeal was a
deficiency.

7. Criminal Law <641.13(7)

Instructions given jury regarding miti-
gating factors were neither ambiguous nor
did they limit the jury to consideration of
only statutorily enumerated mitigating fac-
tors, and thus, had appellate counsel raised
this issue on appeal, it would have been a
frivolous issue.

PER CURIAM.

Marvin Francois appeals the trial court’s
order denying his motion for post-conviction
relief, filed pursuant to Florida Rule of
Criminal Procedure 3.850. Additionally, he
has filed a petition for writ of habeas cor-
pus alleging ineffective assistance of appel-
late counsel. We affirm the denial of the
motion to vacate and deny the petition for
writ of habeas corpus.

Francois was convicted of six first-degree
murders, along with additional crimes, and
received six sentences of death. On direct
appeal, we affirmed his convictions and
death sentences. Francois v. State, 407
So.2d 885 (Fla.1981). The Supreme Court
of the United States denied a petition for
writ of certiorari. Francois v. Florida, ——

FRANCOIS vy. STATE Fla. 359
Cite as, Fla. 423 So.2d 357

U.S. ——, 102 S.Ct. 3511, 73 L.Ed.2d 1384
(1982). On November 5, 1982, the governor
signed a death warrant. Francois’ execu-
tion was scheduled for December 7, 1982.
Francois filed a motion to vacate in the
trial court on November 12, 1982. On No-
vember 15, 1982, the trial court scheduled a
hearing on the motion for November 17. In
the meantime, on November 16, 1982, Fran-
cois filed a petition for habeas corpus in this
Court. Because of the filing of the habeas
petition here, the trial court determined it
had been divested of jurisdiction to consider
the motion for post-conviction relief. Fran-
cois then sought mandamus here to require
the trial court to consider his motion. We
granted his petition for writ of mandamus
and the trial court then considered his mo-
tion, and, finding it to be without merit,
denied it.

APPEAL

On appeal of the denial of his motion to
vacate judgment and sentence, Francois
contends that he was not provided with
effective assistance of counsel at his trial.
He argues that his counsel was ineffective
by: (1) failure to diligently pursue the mo-
tion to dismiss the indictment on the
ground of discriminatory selection of grand
jurors; (2) failure to adequately investigate
appellant’s character and background and
to present nonstatutory mitigating evidence
at the sentencing phase, particularly with
regard to appellant’s mental state; and (3)
failure to object to an instruction given to
the jury concerning the process of weighing
the aggravating and mitigating cireum-
Stances and to request an alternative in-
struction.

[1] In Knight v. State, 394 So.2d 997
(Fla.1981), this Court adopted a four-step
analysis for determining whether an appel-
lant has been provided with reasonably ef-
fective assistance of counsel. The test was
adopted from United States y. DeCoster,
624 F.2d 196 (D.C.Cir.1976) (en bane) and
“provides a means to discover a true miscar-
riage of justice and yet does not place the
judiciary in the role of interfering with
defense counsel's legal and tactical conduct

at trial or on appeal.” Knight vy. State, 394
So.2d at 1000-01. The four-step test re-
quires (1) that the claimant identify a spe-
cific act or omission upon which the claim
of ineffective assistance of counsel is based;
(2) that the appellant show that the specific
act or omission was a substantial and seri-
ous deficiency measurably below the stan-
dard expected of competent attorneys; (3)
that the appellant show that the deficiency,
under the circumstances, was so substantial
as to have probably affected the outcome of
the proceedings; and (4) that if the above
three factors are established, the state still
has the opportunity to rebut the claim of
ineffective assistance by showing beyond a
reasonable doubt that there was in fact no
prejudice. Knight v. State, 394 So.2d at
1001.

Prior to trial, defense counsel moved to
dismiss the indictment on the ground of
discriminatory selection of grand jurors.
He asked for discovery and an evidentiary
hearing. The trial court denied these mo-
tions. On appeal, this Court declined to
consider whether discriminatory selection
had tainted the indictment, finding that
defense counsel had agreed to a ruling
without an evidentiary hearing with knowl-
edge of prior court ruling on the same issue.
Appellant now identifies this action of trial
counsel as an overt act or specific omission
entitling him to relief on the ground of
ineffective assistance.

[2,3] The right of the accused to rea-
sonably competent assistance of legal coun-
sel does not entitle him to have every con-
ceivable constitutional challenge pressed
upon the court. Engle v. Isaac, 456 U.S.
107, 102 S.Ct. 1558, 71 L.Ed.2d 783 (1982).
It was within the range of competence ex-
pected of attorneys in criminal cases for
defense counsel to act as he did under the
circumstances of the case. Counsel's deci-
sion, under the circumstances, was not a
substantial deficiency, measurably below
the standard of performance expected of
competent counsel.

[4] With regard to the claim of failure
to gather and present evidence of mitigat-

Madame Zarina’s life remains an enigma
cloaked in secrecy. Her most devoted disciple,

Lucky dragged her into the living room

how was masked and wore a black jacket.
Two other men, their faces masked with
articles of clothing taken from the rifled
drawers, were going through the pockets
of Stocker and his friends on the floor.

“You tell us what we want to know or
we going to start off with this little girl,”’
‘narled Lucky, pressing the gun barrel
against Margaret's head.

' “Don't hurt her,’ begged Stocker.

“We don’t have anything.”’ He surren-
| dered the key to his locked bedroom and
One of the masked men Opened it and
fntered. He came out with a shotgun.
Poking the barrel at the men on the floor,

he herded them all into Stockcr’s room,.

ordering them to lie down again in a row
n the floor. Through the open door Mar-
8aret saw the room had been ransacked,
the mattress yanked half off the bed.
_ As Stocker and his friends were shoved
Mto the bedroom, one of them protested
Softly to his masked assailant. "We grew
Up together, Bill. Why are you doing
this?””

Just then Margaret heard her boyfriend
holler at the front door. She screamed,

“Don’t hurt him!”
“That must be her boytriend,’’ said

’ one of the masked men. They pushed her

back toward the southeast bedroom and
shoved Michael in that direction, too, or-
dering both to lie face down across the
bed.

Margaret heard shots from Stocker’s
room, several in rapid succession. She
turned her head again and saw a pillow
coming down. She jerked her head away,
but she had seen that Lucky held the pil-
low and a shorter man, stout, with a
bumpy face and pug nose, was holding a
shotgun an inch and a half away. There
was a roar and a blow behind her ear on
the left side.

Her head was buzzing but she held her
breath, trying to play dead. She heard
another shot close, then the men running

- out of the room. She whispered to her

boyfriend. There was no answer.

There was the sound of the front door
opening. The screen door slammed. A car
started up. Margaret held still for several
minutes, but all was silent. She got up and
tiptoed toward the front. Through the
partly open door to Stocker’s room she
could see the row of feet on the floor. But
there was no movement. No sound.

Margaret’s hands were still tied behind
her. She had to turn sideways to reach
around to the front door knob. She
opened the door just a crack and peeked.
Nobody was in sight. Stumbling, sob-
bing, hysterical, she ran next door. The
neighbors called police.

Officer Ronald Drury of the Dade
County Public Safety Department (PSD)
was first to.reach the scene, going initially
to the neighbor’s house from which the
call was made. By this time a crowd of
about 15 people had gathered around the
screaming woman, clustered by the fence
which divided the neighbor’s property
from Stocker’s house. Drury noticed that
the hysterical woman appeared to have
suffered a minor head wound and she
complained about her wrists, thrusting
them out at him. He managed to calm her.
enough to extricate her from the babbling
crowd so she could accompany him next
door.

By that time other police units were
arriving, among them Detectives William
Shinego, David Rivers, Sam Rutledge,
and Sgt. Allen Stanley. The screeching
crowd yelled, ‘‘She’s inside!’ and
“They're all killed!’ as the officers el-
bowed through.

Rivers grabbed the front door handle.
It was locked. He shouted at the woman
approaching with Drury to give him a

key. She had none. It was a snap latch. As
Rivers rared back to kick the door in, he
saw blood on the jam. Then the door flew
open, struck a TV stand and swung shut
again. But in the brief instant, he had
glimpsed a row of bodies on the floor and
one figure upright and moving, partly
concealed behind a partition.

‘One is inside. He’s moving!’ Rivers
shouted. Weapon drawn, he pushed open
the door, ‘Freeze! Police officers!”

“Don’t shoot me again!’’ whimpered a
black man, emerging from behind the par-
tition. Shinego strong-armed him down to
the floor, as the detectives noticed the
man had an ugly head wound, bleeding
profusely, and his wrists were bound be-
hind his back. He was terrified, but lucid.

*‘How many were there?”

**Three.”*

**Black or white?”

**All black.’

“How did they go?”

“They left in the T-bird belonging to
that guy inside next to the wall.”

Rivers radioed for ambulances and the
fire rescue squad. He put out a BOLO on
three black men in a brown T-bird.

Sgt. Stanley and Rutledge had checked
the northeast bedroom where the victims
were laid in a row on the floor. Drury
called from the southeast bedroom,
“There is another one in here!’

Rivers, Rutledge and Shinego were all
on the burglary detail, and by that time
homicide officers were arriving, and
command of the immediate scene was
turned over. The burglary officers moved
outside to help set up a perimeter. Am-
bulances.and the fire emergency squad
sirened in, as well as technicians from the
mobile crime lab.

Homicide Det. Robert Derringer had
been at headquarters when the call came
in about 10:30 p.m., a signal 332 — shoot-
ing with injuries. Cases were taken in ro-
tation and Derringer’s turn was coming
up, so Lt. JamesC. Casey assigned him to
investigate 19335 NW 24th Ave.

Derringer reached the scene about
10:45 finding a heavy traffic jam of police
vehicles, fire engines, ambulances and a

was.

STARKE, Fla., May 28 (AP) — aiar-

vin Francois, convicted as a mass mur-
derer, was denied a further stay ofiex-
ecution today by an appeals court,
clearing the way for his death in,the

-=**ll electric chair early Wednesday.

His lawyers, however, filed a motion
asking the court to reconsider earlier
appeals and were considering an: ‘ap-
peal to the United States Supreme
Court.

The execution has been scheduled for
7 A.M. Wednesday at the Florida ‘State
Prison here.

In tie United States, 42 inmates ‘have
been executed since the death penalty
was reinstated in 1976.

Mr. Francois had been scheduled for
execution Tuesday morning but won a
24-hour stay from the United States
Court of Appeals for the 11th Circuit in
Atlanta. AW

He was convicted in the killing of:six
people in a robbery in the Miami sub-

“HE NEW YORK TIMES, WEDNESDAY, MAY 29, 1985


Florida Inmate Dies for Slayings

STARKE, Fla., May 29 (AP) — Mar-

~~j vin Francois, sentenced to death for

killing six people when his mask fell off
and exposed his face in a robbery, was
executed today in the electric chair.

In his last words, the former heroin
addict said that if an Antichrist existed
it was ‘‘the whole white race.”’

Mr. Francois, a muscular black
man, entered the death chamber at
Florida State Prison and was strapped
in at 7 A.M. The one-minute power
surge was completed at 7:08 A.M. Ten
minutes lapsed before Mr. Francois
was pronounced dead, about four
minutes longer than in past executions.

It was the third execution in Florida
this year, the 13th since the state rein-
stated the death penalty in 1976 and the
44th in the country since capital punish-
ment was reinstated in 1976.

Mr. Francois, 39 years old, was con-
victed in the July 27, 1977, killing of six
people in the robbery of what police
termed a ‘‘drug house’”’ in the Miami
suburb of Carol City.

Late Tuesday the United States Su-
preme Court denied a plea to postpone
the execution. The United States Court
of Appeals for the 11th Circuit denied
Mr. Francois’s motions for a stay
earlier Tuesday.

“I am as a grain of sand on the beach
of the black race,’’ Mr. Francois said in
a final statement he read to witnesses.
‘“‘The black race has lost its pride and
dignity and is slowly dying from within
and without. My death ends my tears,
and the fortune of watching my race

| slowly die.”’

After reading the statement, Mr.
Francois added, ‘“‘If there is such a
thing as an Antichrist, it ain’t one man
but the whole white race.’’

The prisoner was served a breakfast
of lobster, shrimp, ribs, chicken and
french fries.

. Mr. Francois’s last visitors, who left
at 1 A.M., were his mother, Muriel

‘2

if

United Press International
Marvin Francois

Hollingsworth of Miami; his friend
Juanita Pace and his teen-age twins,
Alexis and Aleasian Rolle, said Vernon
Bradford of the Department of Correc-
tions.

Court records indicate that after Mr.
Francois’s mask fell off he said all
eight people who had seen him would
have to be killed. He shot six of the vic-
tims in the head with a shotgun, ac-

cording to testimony. Two others were |

wounded but survived and testified
against Mr. Francois, whose accom-
plices were also sentenced to die.

In a 1982 clemency interview, Mr.
Francois said he never knew his father
well and saw little of his mother. He
testified that he tried heroin at the age
of 16 and he maintained a $300- to $400-
a-day habit in 1976.

Two other Florida inmates are
scheduled to die next Tuesday. They
are Ian Lightbourn, 25, sentenced to
death for a 1981 rape slaying of Nancy
A. O’Farrell of Marion County, and
Oscar Mason, 25, sentenced for stab-
bing Linda Sue Chapman to death in
Tampa in 1980.

GD “Arvin FRANCOIS, a 39 year old black man, is scheduled to be executed on Tuesday,
May 28, in Florida. He would be the third persen executed in Florida this year,
and the 13th since 1977.

Francois wes convicted in 1978, along with three other co-defendants, of the shscting
murder of six black men in what seems to have been a drug dealer related crime. Of
the three other co-defendants, two also received the death penalty, and the third
turned states evidence in exchange for a 20 year sentence for second degree murder.

Francois grew ur in one of the roughest parts of the Miami ghetto. He had a heavy
drug habit which he tried unsuccessfully te kick saveral times. At the rPnalty chase
of his case, he, his mother, an aunt, a cousin, and 3 former employer all testified;
the court found no mitigating circumstances however.

This is his second warrant on this case. His crevious attorney neglected to file
Cert, and he currently has no attorney.

= TEXAS TUESDAY, JUNE 25 12:01 C.S.T.. LETHAL INJECTION

9 CHARLES MILTON, a 34 year old black man, is scheduled to be executed on June 25 for a
murder that took place during a liquor store hold up. He was sentenced to death 1/18/79,

The victim was Mrs. Menaree Denton, a black woman who, along with her husband, owned
and ran the store. Leonard Denton, her husband, was also shot, but he survived and
1 ities i sumone nea testified at the trial. The shooting occurred while Milton, who had been in the
process of robbing the store, got into a struggle with Denton over the gun. Mrs.
Denton came to her husband's assistance, hitting Milton over the head with a

bottle. Milton contends that the gun went off by accident.

Charles Milton had had only one previous conviction (felony theft). However, the
current case occurred as the third robbery in a three day 'spree'. He had a drug
abuse history, and may have been on drugs during the time of the crimes.

Ouring the trial, the prosecutor offered to make a deal for a life sentence, but
Milton turned this down. His attorney presented no evidence and called no witnesses
either during the trial or at the penalty phase.

The case has been through the federal courts and has been denied Cert. There is a
motion for a rehearing on the denial, but it is unlikely to succeed.

After that, the next steop is to ask the Board of Pardons and Parole for a commutation
of sentence to life. In Texas, the Governor must have the recommendation from the Board
before he can act.

POINTS TO MAKE:

a.This was not a deliberately planned murder; there are many indications that it was
an accident.

b.Since being sentenced to death, Charles Milton participated in the program that
allowed certain Death Row inmates to live and work in the general prison population.
(After his execution date became imminent he was moved back to Death Row.) While in
general population, he was not a trouble maker. He got along well and began working
towards his G.E.0. (high school equivalency diploma). Commuting his sentence to life
will not endanger society.

c.The Texas executions, with cheering popcorn eating proponents of death gathered
outside the prison, shows a lessening in the respect for the sacredness of human life.

By LARRY BIVINS
Herald Staff Writer

STARKE — Marvin Francois
aired no feelings of remorse
Wednesday, just resentment
Wednesday before he died in
Florida’s electric chair.

“If there is such a thing as an
anti-Christ, it ain’t one man but
the whole white race,” Francois
said before he became the third
person to die in the chair this year.
While 2,000 volts raced through
Francois’ body, jerking him back-
wards and causing his fists to
clench, the melodic sounds of
Curtis Mayfield’s Ghetto Child
eased through the cool morning air
on the grounds across from Florida
State Prison. Anti-death penalty
protesters said they played it at
Francois’ request.

Francois, 39, sentenced to die
for the murder of six people in a
Carol City home, was pronounced
dead at 7:18 a.m.

He became the 210th person
executed in Florida’s history. He
-was the 13th executed in Florida
since the U.S. Supreme Court
allowed capital punishment to
resume in 1976. All 13 death
warrants were signed by Gov. Bob
Graham.

Graham said Francois’ death
should send a message to all

violence as well as the human

as a result of drugs,” Graham said
in a prepared statement. “I trust
that the strong action taken today
will underscore our determination
to eradicate this scourge.”

convicted of the murders stem-
ming from what police described

previous July. Six of the eight
victims were shot while face
down with their hands tied behind

their: backs.

By ANDREA ROWAND
Associated Press:

STARKE — Blasting the white
race as the “anti-Christ’” killers of
fellow black ‘“scapegoats,’’ mass
murderer Marvin:Francois was elec-
trocuted Wednesday morning for the
1977 execution-style killings of six
Miami-area residents,

“If there is such a thing as the
anti-Christ, it is not one man, but the
whole white race,” Francois said
moments’ after being _ tightly
strapped in the oak electric chair at
_t. Florida State Prison.

, “The black race has lost its pride
and dignity... my death ends my.

tears and years of torture of watch-
ing my race slowly die,” said Fran-
cois, 39, a former heroin addict with
an arrest record dating back to 1968.

He jerked back when the voltage
struck; his hands clenched. Ten
minutes passed before Francois was
declared dead, about four minutes
longer than in past electrocutions.

“Your heart isa strong muscle. It
takes a while to shut down,” ex-
plained Lori Naslund of the Gaines-
ville Medical Examiner’s Office
where an autopsy was performed.
“It did take a little longer but I can’t
say if it was because he was in such

.. See EXECUTE, Page 2B

Execute

e From Page 1B

good shape. It could be because of
his physical condition.”

Judges from the state level to the
US. Supreme Court had denied last-
ditch legal pleas to spare Francois,
one of three men sentenced to die
for murdering six people in a drug-
related robbery in July 1977.

Francois decided the group had
to be killed after his mask fell off,
according to court testimony. The
victims’ hands were tied behind
their backs and they were forced to
lie face-down on the floor.

All eight were shot in the head,
but two survived and helped the

State convict Francois and his co-de- |

fendanis. ;

Another accomplice, Adolphus
Archie, remained in a car outside
the Carol City home. He pleaded
guilty in exchange for a 20-year
prison term.

“Marvin kind of smiled and told
it like the St. Valentine's massacre,”
Archie told jurors.

Francois’ mother had testified in
her son’s defense. “He was a good
son to me. He was good to every-
body,” Muriel Hollingsworth told the
jury.

Hollingsworth was among Fran-
cois’ last visitors Tuesday night. His
girlfriend, Juanita Pace, and teen-
age twins, Alexis and Aleasian Rolle,
also visited with the hulking death
row inmate.

From 1 a.m. Wednesday until
4:30, Francois was alone inside his
death-watch cell, a few steps from
the execution chamber. At 4:30 a.m.,
prison chefs prepared a last meal of

lobster, shrimp, ribs, fried chicken-

and french fries for Francois.
“He ate all but a little bit of the
ribs,” Department of Corrections
spokesman ‘’crnon Bradford said.
Afterward, Fruncois’ head was
shaved to facilitate be.ier electrical
contact. He walked into u:= death

chamber at 7 a.m., and read his
statement from.a sheet of paper
held by.prison Superintendent Rich-
ard Dugger.

“The white Miami institutional-
ized racist judiciary and law en-
forcement systems have made me,
the once heroin addict, the scape-
goat...,” Francois said.

Then a black mask was lowered
over his face. At a nod from Dugger,
the black-hooded, anonymous execu-
tioner — who received $150 cash for
the work — switched the circuit on,
sending 2,000 volts into Francois. He
was pronounced dead at 7:18 a.m.

“The heinous crimes committed
by Marvin Francois underscore the
violence as well as the human trag-
edy which flow into our siate as a re-
sult of drugs,” said Gov. Bob
Graham, who has signed 106 death
warrants since taking office in 1979.
“T trust that the strong action taken
today will underscore our determi-
nation to eradicate this scourge.”

About 25 people, some holding
signs with sayings such as ‘‘Respect
Life, Stop Executions” and “No
More Vengeance, Stop the Death
Penalty,” gathered in front of the
Capitol at noon to protest Francois’
execution.

They .read from the Bible and
prayed as one death penalty sup-
porter stood by with a sign saying,
“God’s Law Commands It. The Gov-
ernor’s Stand on Capital Punishment
Is Just.”

Francois was the 13th death row
inmate executed in Florida since the
U.S. Supreme Court lifted its death
penalty ban in 1976. He was the
third Florida inmate electrocuted
this year.

Two other Florida prisoners are
scheduled to die next Tuesday. They
are Ian Lightbourn, 25, sentenced to
death for the Jan. 16, 1981, rape-
slaying of Nancy A. O'Farrell of
Marion County, and Oscar Mason,
25, sentenced for the March 19,
1980, stabbing death of Linda Sue
Chapman in Tampa.

(90 Fla. 324)

Fla.) DRIGGERS v. STATE 841
(105 So.)

[4] The only other question that can be
considered on this record is whether there
was error in the omission of the trial judge
to instruct the jury that a majority of their
number could recommend the defendant to
merey, which recommendation would have
the effect of reducing the penalty from death
to life imprisonment. Section 6096, Rev.
Gen. Stat.

This court has held that the mere failure
of the trial court to charge the jury that
the statute authorizes a majority of the jury
in capital cases to recommend the accused
to the mercy of the court, is not error.
Washington y. State, 86 Fla. 519, 98.So. 603;
Webster v. State, 47 Fla. 108, 36 So. 584;
Milton y. State, 40 Fla. 251, 24 So. 60. In
each of these cases the charge was murder in
the first degree, the punishment of which
was death. The penalty for the offense with
which the defendant in this case was on
trial is death or imprisonment in the state
prison for life. Section 5051, Rev. Gen.
Stats. So that, even without a recommenda-
tion by the jury, it was in the court’s dis-
cretion to sentence the defendant to life
imprisonment. Clearly, therefore, there was
no error in omitting to charge the jury in
accordance with the statute referred to.

No error having been made to appear, the
judgment is affirmed.

Affirmed.

WHITFIELD, TERRELL, and STRUM,
JJ., concur. '

DRIGGERS v. STATE.

(Supreme Court of Florida, Division B.
Oct. 9, 1925.)

(Syllabus by the Court.)

{. Criminal law @==1182—Conviction for fraud-
ulently altering marks of animal affirmed, in
. absence of error.

No material or harmful error of law or pro-
cedure being made to appear in the record of the
trial, and there being evidence legally sufficient
to sustain the verdict, the judgment herein of
conviction of the statutory offense of fraudu-
lently altering the marks of an animal with in-
tent to claim the same is affirmed.

2. Points decided in prior case are stated in
headnotes.

The points decided in the case of Hart v.
State (Fla.) 103 So. 633, are stated in the head-
notes.

3. Criminal law @=-1169(1), 1170(1), 1172(1),
{173(1)—Errors in giving or refusing instruc-
tions and admission or rejection of testimony
not reaching legality of trial not ground for re-
versal.

Alleged errors in giving or refusing charges
or instructions, and in the admission or rejec-
tion of testimony which do not weaken the effect
of the admitted testimony, and which do not

reach the legality of the trial itself, will not be
considered grounds for reversal, where the evi-
dence leaves no room for reasonable doubt of
the defendant’s guilt.

4. Criminal law @—1159(2), (169(1), 1170(1),
1172(1), 1173(1)—Errors in admission of or
rejection of evidence in giving or refusing
charges or procedure not ground for reversal,
unless they injuriously affect substantial
rights; judgment not reversed because ver-
dict is not sustained by evidence unless clear-
ly wrong.

The judgment should not be reversed or a
new trial granted in any case, civil or criminal,
for errors in rulings upon the admission or re-
jection of evidence, or for errors in giving or re-
fusing charges, or for errors in any other matter
of procedure or practice, unless it shall appear
to the court from a consideration of the entire
cause that such errors injuriously affect the
substantial rights of the complaining party. Nor
should a judgment be reversed or a new trial
granted on the ground that the verdict is not
sustained by the evidence, unless it appears that
there was no substantial evidence to support the
finding, or that upon the whole evidence the
verdict is clearly wrong, or that the jury were
not governed by the evidence in making their
finding.

Error to Circuit Court, Dixie County; M.
F. Horne, Judge.

George Driggers was convicted of fraud-
ulently altering the marks of an animal with
intent to claim it, and he brings error. Af-
firmed.

W. P. Chavous, of Cross City, for plaintiff
in error.

Rivers Buford, Atty. Gen., and Marvin
C. Mcintosh, Asst. Atty. Gen., for the State.

PER CURIAM. [1] The conviction herein
of the statutory offense of fraudulently al-
tering the marks of an animal with intent to
claim same (section 5176, Rev. Gen. Stats.
1920) is in accord with controlling rules of
law as to the charges given. See Atzroth v.
State, 10 Fla. 207; Williams v. State, 40
Fla. 480, text 483, 25 So. 143, 74 Am. St. Rep.
154; as to charges refused being covered
by charges given, Groover v. State, 82 Fla.
427, 90 So. 4738, 26 A. L. R. 375; as to ad-
mission of objectionable evidence where it is
in substance otherwise admitted in evidence,
Mercer vy. State,'83 Fla. 555, 92 So. 5385; as
to general objections to evidence, Flores v.
State, 72 Fla. 302, 73 So. 234, L. R. A. 1917B,
1148; as to evidence of other independent
acts tending to show intent, Wallace v. State,
41 Fla. 547, 26 So. 718; as to evidence ob-
tained on arrest, State ex rel. Stillman v.
Merritt, 86 Fla. 164, 99 So. 230.

The evidence is legally sufficient to sus-
tain the verdict, and no material errors ap-
pear. See Shuler v. State, 84 Fla. 414, 93 So.
672.

“LU USA

URGENT ACTION PROGRAM OFFICE « P.O. Box 1270 « Nederland, CO 80466-1270 « 303-440-0913 « FAX: 303-258-7881
TLX 34/91 - Death Penalty 3_June 1991

USA (Florida): Bobby FRANCIS

Bobby Francis, aged 46, is scheduled to be executed in Florida on 18 June
1991. Francis, black, was convicted of the 1975 murder of a black male.
Both were allegedly involved in drug-related activity. Francis was tried
three times for the crime. At his third trial the jury recommended that he
be sentenced to life imprisonment. The judge overruled the jury’s
recommendation and instead sentenced Bobby Francis to death on 29 March

1983.

According to reports, Bobby Francis has been diagnozed as suffering. from
fetal alcohol syndrome after his mother, an alcoholic, drank heavily while

pregnant.

. Governor Bob Martinez deniéd Bobby Francis’ request for executive clemency
some years ago. Florida’s present Governor, Lawton Chiles, will be asked to
reconsider the case in light of the fact.that he has set up a task force to
look into the “jury override" issue.

BACKGROUND INFORMATION

Florida is one of only three US states which permit the trial judge to
overrule the jury’s sentencing recommendation. Florida’s "override"
provision was upheld as constitutional by the US Supreme Court in 1984. In
‘more than 100 capital trials since 1973 judges in Florida have ignored the
jury’s recommendation of life imprisonment and imposed the death penalty
(judges have overriden toward mercy in only one-tenth as many cases). Many
such death sentences are later reversed on appeal, but in September 1984
Ernest Dobbert was executed even though a jury had voted 10-to-2 against
the death penalty. And in August 1988 Florida executed Beauford White
despite his trial jury’s unanimous recommendation that he receive a life
sentence.

Amnesty International takes no pesition on whether sentencing decisions
should rest with judges or juries. However, the overriding of a
recommendation of life imprisonment by a consensus of jurors, reached after
their consideration of the facts, undermines the principle that death
should not be imposed as a penalty where there is any doubt as to its
appropriateness in a particular case.

As of 24 April 1991 there were 295 prisoners under sentence of death in
Florida: the third largest state death row population in the country.
Florida has executed 26 prisoners since 1976. The most recent was Roy
Harich on 24 April 1991. The method of execution is electrocution.

Amnesy International opposes the death penalty in all cases as a violation
of the right to life and the right not to be subjected to cruel, inhuman
and degrading treatment or punishment, as proclaimed in the Universal
Declaration of Human Rights.

This Urgent Action appeal originated from Amnesty International's research headquarters at the International Secretariat in London, United
Kingdom. Amnesty International is an independent worldwide movement working for the international protection of human rights. It seeks the
release of men and women detained anywhere because of their beliefs, color, sex, ethnic origin. language or religious creed, provided they have not
used nor advocated violence. These are termed prisoners of conscience. It works for fair and prompt trials for all political prisoners and works on

behaif of such people detained without charge or trial. It opposes the death penalty and torture or other cruel, inhuman or degrading treatment or
Punishment of all prisoners without reservation.

BBE:

Florida Executes Informer ’s Killer

Hours After High Court Decision

STARKE, Fla., June 25 (AP) — A
man who had tried to inject a drug in-
former with Drano and battery acid
and then shot him to death was exe-
cuted today in the electric chair at the
state prison here.

The - 46-year-old prisoner, Bobby
Marion Francis, was put to death less
than 24 hours after the United States
Supreme Court sharply limited the
right of inmates to pursue Federal
court appeals after missing deadlines
for appealing their cases in state court.

He had come within four hours of ex-
ecution last week before a Federal ap-
peals court in Atlanta granted a stay.

The stay was lifted after the Supreme|’

Court’s ruling on Monday.
_ Mr. Francis was sentenced in 1983 to
die for the 1975 slaying of Titus R. Wal-

-ters, an informer in a drug investiga-
-tion that resulted in Mr. Francis’s ar-

rest. The judge at his trial overruled
the jury’s recommendation of life im-
prisonment.

Mr. Francis tortured Mr. Walters for
two hours by trying to inject him with
Drano and battery acid. When that
failed, he shot Mr. Walters twice.in the
head. When Mr. Walters remained con-
scious, Mr. Francis. shot him. point-
blank inthe heart. >

Mr. Francis was the 27th person in
Florida and the 148th in the United
States to be executed since the Su-

preme Court in 1976 allowed states to
resume capital punishment.

He was declared dead at 7:07 A.M.,
about three minutes after an execu-
tioner threw a switch on the electric
chair, sending 2,000 volts through Mr.
Francis’s body.

Mr. Francis, a Muslim, began his last
statement by chanting in Arabic,
“There is no God but Allah, and Mo-
hammed is his prophet.”

Muhammad, joined with about a dozen
death-penalty opponents at a protest
vigil in a pasture outside the prison. He
said Mr. Francis had accepted his fate

“as Allah’s will? © .:

might have. done,’’. Mr. Muhammad
said, ‘but he’s never been one to con-
fess.”

Susan Cary, a Gainesville lawyer
who works with death-row prisoners,
said Mr. Francis was stunned Monday
when told that the stay had been lifted.

After winning the stay, Mr. Francis
had been returned to a regular death-
row cell from a-holding cell where pris-
oners facing imminent execution are
kept and had been told that he would
not be executed on this warrant, his
third. ‘To me, that is torture,’ Ms.
Cary said.

federal court appeals after missing
deadlines for appealing their cases

in state court.

wea stay. The stay was lifted after the

mhigh court’s ruling Monday. .

PER CURIAM.

This cause is before the Court on appeal
of a judgment of the Circuit Court of the
Eleventh Judicial Circuit, Dade County,
which adjudicated the appellant guilty of
six counts of murder in the first degree,
along with additional crimes, pursuant to
which six sentences of death were imposed
upon him. This Court has jurisdiction of
his appeal. Art. V, § 3(b)(1), Fla.Const.

The appellant was convicted on six counts
of first-degree murder, two counts of at-
tempted first-degree murder, and three
counts of robbery. The evidence showed
that in the early evening of July 27, 1977,
John Errol Ferguson gained admittance to
the home of Livingston Stocker in the Carol
City section of Miami by posing as a power
company employee. Margaret Wooden was
present in the home at the time. After

entering, Ferguson bound Wooden's hands
and blindfolded her. Then he admitted
Beauford White and the appellant Marvin
Francois. The three men searched the
house for valuables, taking some firearms
and some of Margaret Wooden’s jewelry. ,
Then the three men covered their faces
with masks.

Shortly thereafter Livingston Stocker ar-
rived home with five friends—Henry Clay-
ton, Johnny Hall, Randolph Holmes, Charles
Stinson, and Gilbert Williams. The intrud-
ers tied the six men up and took their
valuables. Still later Michael Miller, Liv-
ingston Stocker’s nephew, arrived at the
house. He too was tied up and robbed.

The appellant’s mask then slipped off and
he declared that all the victims would have
to be killed. Ferguson took Margaret
Wooden and Michael Miller into a bedroom
and shot them both in the head with a
pistol. Appellant Francois took the other
six victims into another bedroom, made
them lie down on the floor, and shot them
each in the head with a shotgun.

After the three attackers departed, Mar-
garet Wooden ran to a neighbor’s house to
call for help. When the police arrived they
found Johnny Hall crawling toward the
back door of the house. Both Wooden and
Hall survived and testified at trial. The
other six victims died. At trial, Johnny
Hall identified the appellant as the man
who shot him and five others with a shot-
gun. Theresa Rolle testified that Francois
admitted to her his participation in the
murders.

Adolpus Archie testified that he took
Francois, Ferguson, and White to Stocker’s
home in his car and waited for them down
the street. He testified further that it was
unnecessary for him to pick the other three
up and drive them away since they depart-
ed the scene in Stocker’s car. Archie met
the other three later and assisted in dispos-
ing of evidence. He testified that appellant
Francois told him that the real purpose of
the venture was not robbery but murder.
Francois stated further, according to Ar-
chie’s testimony, that he and Ferguson had


¢

884 Ala.

In dealing with this exception, some of
the cases resort to the phrase “unless he
abuses his discretion.” Strictly speaking,
this phrase should read, “unless the trial
judge attempts to exercise discretion when
no discretion is permitted under the law.”
I have found no Alabama case, and the
majority opinion cites none, that applies the
abuse of discretion exception to grant a
writ of mandamus where the trial judge
had the right to exercise his judicial discre-
tion. See State Board of Administration v.
Roquemore, 218 Ala. 120, 117 So. 757 (1928).
This should not be surprising, for a literal
interpretation of the phrase “unless he abu-
ses his discretion” would effectively destroy
the essential substantive distinction be-
tween the right of review by appeal and by
petition for writ of mandamus. Denson v.
First National Bank of Birmingham, 276
Ala. 146, 159 So.2d 849 (1964).

To review by mandamus the propriety of
the trial court’s order granting defendant’s
motion for a new trial by evaluating its
merits is the identical standard for review
by appeal. Once the need for the right of
appeal is thus eliminated, the elements that
constitute mandamus as an extraordinary
writ are lost and the two methods of review
equate; and, thus, we have usurped by case
law the statutory and rule-making preroga-
tive to establish the right of review by
appeal.

One final observation: I personally initia-
ted an addition to the proposed Rules of
Criminal Procedure to provide the State the
right of appeal from an order granting the
defendant a new trial. The Rules as pro-
posed by the Special Committee limited the
State’s right of appeal to certain adverse
pretrial rulings. The ABA Standards of
Criminal Justice are not so limited. Indeed,

510 (1945), in which the Court, by writ of
mandamus, compelled the trial court to apply
the venue statute in a divorce action according
to its express terms.

3. The majority’s application of “abuse of dis-
cretion” in this motion for new trial context is
misplaced, resulting from an ill conceived con-
cern over the validity of a double jeopardy plea
upon retrial. This unfortunate prejudgment of
the ultimate outcome of the case produces an

407 SOUTHERN REPORTER, 2d SERIES

I would have no objection to adopting a
rule per the instant case for prospective
application, giving the State the right to
review by appeal a trial court’s order grant-
ing the defendant a new trial. Such a
holding would be entirely proper, though, in
my opinion, the criminal rules are the more
appropriate means of promulgating and ap-
plying such a procedural change in our law.

But this discussion is of purely academic
interest, for a rule allowing appeals by the
State is no longer necessary. Henceforth,
the right of review of an order granting a
new trial is available by way of petition for
a writ of mandamus. To be sure, the State
has gained much more than that: Where, as
here, the writ is granted summarily by the
Court of Criminal Appeals and this Court
denies the petition for writ of certiorari or
affirms the appellate court’s order, the
State succeeds in setting aside the order
granting a new trial and reinstating the
judgment of conviction and sentence with-
out giving the defendant the benefit of an
in-depth consideration as mandated for re-
view of cases by appeal.

SHORES, Justice (dissenting):

I dissent. I cannot agree that a circuit

judge in granting a new trial because he

feels “that an injustice has probably been

done” is “guilty of usurpation of power.” I
would, therefore, overrule the application
for rehearing.

erroneous result oriented holding and is based
upon a misinterpretation of Hudson v. Louisi-
ana [1981], 450 U.S. 40, 101 S.Ct. 970, 67
L.Ed.2d 30 (1981). This is a clear case of a trial
judge’s entertaining personal doubts about the
verdict and acting to prevent a perceived injus-
tice. Hudson bars a second trial only where
the new trial motion’s ground of insufficiency
of the evidence is sustained.

FRANCOIS vy. STATE Fla. 885
Cite as, Fla., 407 So.2d 885

Marvin FRANCOIS, Appellant,
v.

STATE of Florida, Appellee.
No. 54461.

Supreme Court of Florida.

Oct. 15, 1981.
Rehearing Denied Jan. 28, 1982.

Defendant was convicted in the Circuit
Court, Dade County, Richard S. Fuller, J.,
of six counts of first-degree murder, two
counts of attempted first-degree murder,
and three counts of robbery, and upon be-
ing sentenced to death, defendant appealed.
The Supreme Court held that: (1) right to
appeal denial of motions for discovery, for a
hearing, and to dismiss indictment for rea-
son of alleged systematic exclusion of per-
sons of a particular race from grand jury
was waived by defendant by failing to dili-
gently pursue matter and by inducing trial
court to rule against him when a favorable
ruling would have resulted, possibly, in con-
vening of a new grand jury and issuance of
a new indictment or at least in a delay of
trial for purposes of discovery or evidentia-
ry hearing or both and (2) although trial
court’s sentencing errors resulted in recita-
tion of three statutory aggravating circum-
stances that were not properly established
by evidence, sentences of death were never-
theless warranted, where defendant, twice
previously convicted of felonies which in-
volved use or threat of violence, was con-
victed of six premeditated, execution style
murders committed in course of committing
robbery and for purpose of eliminating wit-
nesses to crimes of robbery, murders were
especially heinous, atrocious, and cruel,
there were four statutory aggravating cir-
cumstances and there were no mitigating
circumstances.

Affirmed.

Sundberg, C. J., dissented and filed
opinion.

1. Homicide = 253(1), 256
Robbery <24.1(1)

Evidence, including testimony of indi-
viduals who survived robberies and shoot-
ings which were fatal to six others, was
sufficient to sustain convictions of six
counts of first-degree murder, two counts
of attempted first-degree murder, and three
counts of robbery.

2. Criminal Law <=938(1)

A new trial based on assertion that
testimony of alibi witness was newly discov-
ered evidence and that State suppressed
information concerning identity and where-
abouts of witness was not warranted since,
if anyone had known about witness and her
possible value to defendant for alibi pur-
poses, it would have been the defendant
and, in any event, alleged alibi testimony
would not have been probative because evi-
dence showed that crimes were committed
well before times with which alibi testimo-
ny was concerned.

3. Indictment and Information <=144
Rule providing that motion to dismiss
must be made either before or upon ar-
raignment unless court grants further time,
rather than statute providing that a chal-
lenge or objection to grand jury may not be
made. after it has been empaneled and
sworn, was applicable in case, and motion to
dismiss, based on alleged systematic exclu-
sion of persons of particular race from
grand jury which returned indictment, was
untimely where court granted defendant
additional time in which to file his motion.
West’s F.S.A. § 905.05; 33 West’s F.S.A.
Rules of Criminal Procedure, Rule 3.190(c).

4. Criminal Law <> 1026

Right to appeal denial of motions for
discovery, for a hearing, and to dismiss in-
dictment for reason of alleged systematic
exclusion of persons of a particular race
from grand jury was waived by defendant
by failing to diligently pursue matter and
by inducing trial court to rule against him
when a favorable ruling would have result-
ed, possibly, in convening of a new grand
jury and issuance of a new indictment or at
least in a delay of trial for purposes of

888 Fla. 407 SOUTHERN REPORTER, 2d SERIES

done the shooting. Archie pled guilty to
second-degree murder and received a sen-
tence of twenty years imprisonment.

Prior to trial, appellant moved to dismiss
the indictment on the ground that it was
issued by a grand jury selected under a
procedure that systematically excludes mi-
norities from participation. He moved also
for an evidentiary hearing on the motion,
and for discovery of the master list from
which grand jurors are drawn.

{1] Appellant, in essence, presents three
issues on appeal of the judgments of convic-
tion. He contends first that the evidence
presented at trial was insufficient to sup-
port the judgments of conviction. We find,
however, that the judgments were rendered
pursuant to jury verdicts that were based
on competent, substantial evidence. Appel-
lant’s first argument is without merit.

[2] Secondly, appell*nt argues that he
should have been granted a new trial be-
cause of newly discovered evidence and be-
cause the evidence in question was known
to the state at the time of trial but not
disclosed to him. Appellant’s motion for
new trial alleged that shortly before sen-
tencing a woman named Sheila Walker ap-
proached defense counsel and said that ap-
pellant was with her at 11:00 p.m. on the
night of the murders. The motion asserted
that this was newly discovered evidence and
that the state had suppressed information
concerning the identity and whereabouts of
this witness. If anyone had known about
this witness and her possible value to the
accused for alibi purposes, however, it
would have been the defendant. Further-
more, the evidence showed that the mur-
ders were committed well before 11:00 p.m.,
so the testimony it was alleged the witness
would give would not have been probative
and would not have changed the outcome.
See United States v. Agurs, 427 U.S. 97, 96
S.Ct. 2392, 49 L.Ed.2d 342 (1976); Thomas
v. State, 374 So.2d 508 (Fla.1979), cert. de-
nied, 445 U.S. 972, 100 S.Ct. 1666, 64
L.Ed.2d 249 (1980). Therefore, appellant’s
contentions on this issue are without merit.

Thirdly, appellant contends that the judg-
ments must be reversed because to try a
defendant under an indictment returned by
a grand jury from which persons of a par-
ticular race have been systematically ex-
cluded denies equal protection of the law.
Appellant’s motion to dismiss alleged that
there had been for a number of years a
substantial disparity between the percent-
age of blacks and Hispanics in Dade Coun-
ty’s eligible population and the percentage
serving on grand juries. He contends that
in order for him to have made out a prima
facie case of discriminatory selection, which
would have required a showing of justifica-
tion from the state, he needed to have ac-
cess to the grand jury master list and also
should have been afforded an evidentiary
hearing.

[3] Appellant argues that his affidavits
and proffered exhibits demonstrated a dis-
parity that was unlikely to be due to
chance, thus entitling him to discovery of
the master list and an evidentiary hearing.
See Castanda v. Partida, 480 U.S. 482, 97
S.Ct. 1272, 51 L.Ed.2d 498 (1977); Dykman
v. State, 294 So.2d 633 (Fla.1974); Rojas v.
State, 288 So.2d 234 (Fla.1973); Seay v.
State, 286 So.2d 532 (Fla.1978), cert. denied,
419 U.S. 847, 95 S.Ct. 84, 42 L.Ed.2d 77
(1974). In response the state argues that
the appellant waived his right to raise this
issue by not timely filing his motion. The
state refers us to section 905.05, Florida
Statutes (1977), which provides:

A challenge or objection to the grand
jury may not be made after it has been
empaneled and sworn. This section shall
not apply to a person who did not know
or have reasonable ground to believe, at
the time the grand jury was empaneled
and sworn, that cases in which he was or
might be involved would be investigated
by the grand jury.

We find that this statute does not apply to
the instant case. Rather, we conclude that
the time limit for the filing of the motion to
dismiss was governed by Florida Rule of
Criminal Procedure 3.190(c), which provides
that a motion to dismiss is to be made
either before or upon arraignment unless

FRANCOIS v. STATE Fla. 889
Cite as, Fla., 407 So.2d 885

the court grants further time. The motion
here was timely filed because the court
granted the appellant additional time in
which to file his motion.

[4] Although we find that the motion
was timely filed, we hold that appellant
waived his right to appeal the denial of his
motions for discovery, for a hearing, and to
dismiss the indictment, by failing to dili-
gently pursue the matter and by inducing
the trial court to rule against him. The
record shows that appellant’s motion came
up for consideration at a pre-trial hearing
held January 3, 1978, at which other mo-
tions were argued. At that hearing appel-
lant’s counsel stated:

The only other motion that I filed is
the motion to dismiss the Grand Jury
indictment based on the Blue Ribbon
Grand Jury and requesting an evidentia-
ry hearing on that point, but it—I am
going to forego any arguments on that.
In fact, I am not going to ask for a
hearing on it until a later date simply
because I do not think there is any way I
can get this case to trial by next week.

Transcript of Proceedings, vol. I, at 12-13.
Further pre-trial hearings were held on
February 21 and 23, 1978, and again the
matter was not brought up by defense
counsel. On February 27 defense counsel
declared that he was ready for trial. How-
ever, the trial was not finally scheduled to
begin until April 17. On that day defense
counsel, Mr. Diamond, brought the motion
to dismiss to the court’s attention and the
following exchange ensued:

MR. DIAMOND: Your Honor, just be-
fore we start on the motion to suppress, I
am not sure what pretrial motions have
at this point been granted and have not,
because of the fact that we have three
defendants. Now there are four. Well,
four defendants have been charged
throughout. ‘

There were motions to adopt based on
all motions previously filed. I filed a
motion to dismiss the grand jury indict-
ment in this case originally. I believe it
was dismissed—I mean, it was not dis-
missed by this Court. It was dismissed in

other courts with regard to these defend-
ants. This Court did not dismiss the in-
dictments against them.

THE COURT: That is true. I find the
grand jury was legally constituted, and I
deny the motions.

MR. DIAMOND: Fine.

Transcript of Proceedings, vol. I, at 40.

This exchange reveals that defense coun-
sel practically invited the court to rule
against him. A favorable ruling on the
motion would have resulted, possibly, in the
convening of a new grand jury and the
issuance of a new indictment, or at least in
a delay of the trial for purposes of dis-
covery or an evidentiary hearing or both.
Yet the record also reveals that appellant
and his counsel did not press for a pretrial
ruling because they did not want to delay
the trial. The defense tactic was to delay
the deciding of the claim of grand jury
illegality until just before trial, in hopes of
upsetting the conviction on appeal. See
Francis v. Henderson, 425 U.S. 536, 96 S.Ct.
1708, 48 L.Ed.2d 149 (1976); Davis v. Unit-
ed States, 411 U.S. 233, 93 S.Ct. 1577, 36
L.Ed.2d 216 (1973). We cannot condone
such tactics and therefore refuse to consider
this issue on appeal.

Finding no merit in any of appellant’s
challenges to the convictions, we affirm
them.

We come now to consideration of the
sentencing proceeding and sentences of
death. Appellant presents a series of argu-
ments with regard thereto.

(5] Appellant argues that section 921.-
141, Florida Statutes (1977), violates due
process by arbitrarily establishing a pre-
sumption that death is the appropriate sen-
tence in a felony murder situation, because
the existence of the underlying felony can
be used to aggravate the capital felony.
Appellant has no standing to make this
argument since the evidence shows that he
was the actual perpetrator of five premedi-
tated murders and was a first-degree prin-
cipal in one other murder. Moreover, we
recently rejected this argument when it
was presented by an appellant who has

The courts have begun to ex-

da Supreme Court rejected White’s
last appeal with a terse comment
that it was filed too late.

One participant likened the
crime to a “St. Valentine’s Day Mas-
sacre.” White accompanied two
men to a Carol City house on July
27, 1977, hoping to find drugs, jewel-
ry and other valuables.

The threesome tied up the wom-
an who answered the door. When
the house’s owner came home with
friends, the intruders made every-
one lie on the floor and shot all
eight in the back of the head. Two
survived.

Another participant in the slay-
ings, Marvin Francois, was executed
in 1985. }

By LUCY MORGAN
Times Staff Writer

STARKE — Convicted murderer
Beauford White heaved a deep sigh,
curled his fingers and died Friday in
Florida’s electric chair. He was the
213th person to be executed - :
by the state since 1924.

White, 41, died facing a
room filled with witnesses,
including prosecutors, law
enforcement officers, re-
porters and Larry Spalding,

‘head of the state agency

that defends death-row pris-
opers.

. White took one long, ex-
pressionless’ look at the
crowd and sat in the electric chair,
lowering his eyes to watch prison

Officials strap him in.

._A tiny’ spark of fire glimmered

" Momentarily on the edge of a black

mud-flap-like mask that shielded his
face from view. after he was strapped
into the chair. White appeared to
heave a big sigh as 2,000 volts of
electricity surged through his ‘body.
About two minutes later his body
sagged a bit as the power was shut off
and doctors began examining him. He
was pronounced dead a moment later.

“The sentence of the state of
Florida against Beauford White was

St lake. Temes

after 10-year fight for life

carried out at 7:11 a.m.,” said Lt. Don
Gladish, who remained on a line to the
office of Gov. Bob Martinez while
White was led into the chamber and
executed.

Outside the prison in a cow pas-
ture across. from the Florida State
Prison, a handful of death-penalty

protesters held lighted can-
dles.

“Only the born losers
die here,” said Mike Martin,
a University of Florida stu-
dent from Iowa who said he
has been here to protest the

last three éxecutions.
A short distance away
&! stood Sheila Lee, a Lake
_ Butler woman who came to
show support for the death

penalty:

Spalding, appearing subdued after
watching his client die, said White
would never have been executed in
other states because the jury that .
convicted him of murder unanimously
recommended life in prison. A judge
overrode the Dade County jury’s rec-
ommendation and sentenced him to
die.

“In this whole process, with a
room fyll of witnesses, reporters and
guards, there was only one black par-
ticipant and that was Beauford

_ FLORIDA'S HANGING CONTRIBUTION,

A Murderer Who Said He Was Going to Eat
His Linner in Heaven.

One of the most notorious desperadoes that
Florida has ever known paid the penalty of
his crimes on the gallows on April! 22, at Mon-

! tieello.

On the 4th of last December Mr. J. H.

| Whitaker was shot and killed by a negro
| named Andrew Fell. After some time Fell

was captured, tried and found guilty. After
being brought from the jail.and placed on the
scaffold he talked to the crowd in and outside
the enclosure for some time. He denied his
cuilt to the last; said he had made his peace
with God and would go straight to heaven. He
begged his family and friends to meet him in
heaven:

“ Well, I eat breakfast here,” he said, “but I

‘shall eat my dinner in heaven. As soon as the

rope is chopped I will be gone.”

He called for some of his friends and told
them he wanted them to pyt his old frame in
the ground, and he wanted them to fix up his
fencés- to keep the hogs out and tend his
crop for his "Wifeeand children. He then
thanked God that he had no blood on his
hands; said he wanted to go, so he could tell
God about being wrapped up in chains, and
about a young lady climbing the fence and
laughing athim. He then said he had nothing
more to. say. Rev. Mr. Pasco led in prayer.
The assemblage then sang “On Jordan’s
Stormy Banks I Stand.”

At 8 minutes to 12 o’clock the rope was cut,
the eculprit’s neck being broken by the fall,
After struggling a minute or se he became
quiet, and in thirteen minutes he was de-
clared dead by Dr. Theodore Turnbull.

Fell was a notorious thieffand of a very vio-
lent disposition. He had repeatedly evaded
arrest: by resisting the officers of the law. On
the 18th of December last the Sheriff deputed
among others Mr. J. H. Whitaker to assist him
in arresting Fell, who was charged with nu-
merous thefts and who was then i:: hiding.
An old negro woman notified Fell that a Sher-

he was concealed and that they would attempt

loaded a double-barrelled shot-gun and lay in

Florida then offered $3,000“for the arrest of

friends of Whitaker. After a two months’

condition. His feet were so badly frostbitten
that he was unable to stand up. According to
his own statement he had been dragging him-
self about on-.all fours for. more than two
months. He made this statement as an apolo-
gy for his capture without violent resistance.

Fell was a very black negro, about. forty

Lottie's Darling Doetor is Assaulted by Her

The Franklin House, Lawrence, Mass., was
the scene of a lively sensation a few evenings
ago. For several: days, a clairvoyant, known
as Lottie J. Darling, occupied apartments in
that hotel in company with Dr. Charles Averill,
who claims to be a magnetic healer. Lottie’s
real name is said to be Lillie E. Hersey. She
is about 25 years of age and of quite prepos-
sessing appearance. Her husband traveled
with her two years, acting as agent, but six
months ago she deserted him and formed a
semi-copartnership with Dr. Averill. The
jealous husband traeed them to this city, and.
arriving at the Franklin House, proceeded to

must be paid by her.

NORTH CAROLINA GIVES ITS QUOTA.

A Wife Murderer Expiates His Offense on
the Gallows—-Details of the Crime.

Abram Martin was hanged at Abbeville, N. C.,
on Friday last for thé murder of his wife.

Lucinda Martin was slain by her husband on
the 15th of last July at her home near Millway.
Everything connected with the tragedy makes
it one of peculiar horror. Abram Martin was
about sixty years of age. He was not far re-
moved from being an absolute idiot. Abram
and Lucinda were common laborers and were
not thrifty, as they have accumulated nothing
since emancipation. They had been married
thirty years and she had borne him fifteen
children. On the night of the 15th of July they
retired in friendship, but a difference arose be-
tween them toward morning. Lacinda, it
seems, left the house; and went out into the
yard. Before she returned Abram also left the
room, and was busy splitting some kindling
wood for his morning fire with hie axe. As she
passed him on her way into the house he struck
her on the head with his axe, felling her tothe
ground. In order to make success a certainty
he struck her two more blows when she was
down. She languished for two days, and then

tinct impressions of the butt of the axe upon
her skull. ae
Abram gave the alarm by loud cries, and the
children rushed o@fof the house and neigh-
bors were soon upon the scene. Abram was
holding the head of his murdered wife in his
arms and repeating, in apparent distress and

grief, “Somebody has killed my poor wife.”

laughing at him. He then said he had nothing

reporter had told him that a State witness,
Deborah Wesley, had been charged with
second degree murder two weeks earlier.
The homicide occurred in Monroe County,
Florida and was unrelated to Francis’ case.
Defendant’s counsel urged that the prose-
cutor’s failure to disclose this arrest was
not only a discovery violation but constitut-
ed prosecutorial misconduct. Defense
counsel requested an opportunity to depose
the witness and to investigate the case.
The prosecutor responded that he had not
spoken to Wesley about her pending
charges, and did not wish to in order to
avoid the appearance of giving her favor-
able treatment. The prosecutor suggested
that defense counsel could only inquire into
whether the State had made Wesley any
“offers, deals or promises” for her testimo-
ny. Additionally, the prosecutor stated
that he was under no duty to disclose the
incident because he knew of no evidence
favorable to the accused.

The trial court found no prosecuto. al
misconduct and denied an adjournment of:
the trial, suggesting that Wesley’s deposi-
tion could be taken later—the day after the
completion of jury selection. The State
moved to prevent the defense from ques-
tioning Wesley about the charges pending
against her. The court did not limit the
parameters of the deposition but stated
that he would consider the State’s motion
in limine after the deposition was taken.

Two days later, after Wesley had been
examined and cross-examined at trial, de-
fense counsel again sought to revisit the
court’s ruling concerning the murder
charge. He acknowledged that he had dis-
covered no information concerning any spe-
cific deal made by the State, but proffered
his desire to cross-examine Wesley as to
the charge so that the jury could consider
her truthfulness, bias, and possible motive
for testifying. The trial court denied the
proffer. At the conclusion of the trial, the
prosecutor noted that Wesley’s deposition
had been taken and that she had answered

5. Although the trial judge directed the court
clerk to file the deposition transcript as a trial
exhibit, no such exhibit is contained in any part
of the voluminous record before this court.

FRANCIS v. DUGGER , 475
Cite as 697 F.Supp. 472 (S.D.Fla. 1988) ,

questions under oath regarding any prom-

ises, threats or coercion from the State.®
She had declined to answer questions about

the specific incident of the second degree

murder charge. Defense counsel again
stated that he wanted to bring the murder
charge to the jury’s attention so that they
could determine whether she was testifying
to promote her own best interests.®

[1-3] When the defendant’s guilt or in-
nocence may turn on the reliability of a
witness, the prosecutor’s nondisclosure of
evidence affecting the credibility or bias of
the witness may deprive the accused of the
guarantees of the Confrontation Clause of
the sixth amendment. Particularly rele-
vant here is the well-recognized function of
eross-examination to elicit and expose a
witness’ motivation for testifying. Davis
». Alaska, 415 U.S. 308, 316-17, 94 S.Ct.
1105, 1110-11, 94 S.Ct. 1105, 39 L.Ed.2d
347 (1974). However, the Confrontation
Clause protects the opportunity for effec-
tive cross-examination, not any and all
cross-examination the defense may want to
pursue. Delaware v. Fensterer, 474 US.
15, 106 S.Ct. 292, 88 L.Ed.2d 15 (1985).
The sixth amendment does not prevent a
trial judge from imposing reasonable limi-
tations on cross-examination when bal-
ancing concerns of harassment, prejudice,
and confusion or lack of relevancy. Dela-
ware v. Van Arsdali, 475 U.S. 678, 106
S.Ct. 1431, 1435, 89 L.Ed.2d 674 (1986).

[4,5] The State argues that there was
no relevance or “logical nexus” between
the pending second degree murder charge
and Wesley’s possible bias or motive for
testifying. Although the witness was
cross-examined as to other matters, it can-
not be said that this area of inquiry was
not critical to the jury evaluation of her
motive for testifying, in view of imminence
and seriousness of the second degree mur-
der charge. The trial judge prohibited all
inquiry into the possibility that Wesley’s
motivation in testifying was anticipation
that her charge would either not be in-

6. Wesley was later convicted of second degree
murder of her husband and received a thirty-
year sentence. Evans v. State, 452 So.2d 987
(Fla. 3d DCA 1984).

ORDER DENYING PETITION FOR
WRIT OF HABEAS CORPUS

NESBITT, District Judge.

This cause is before the court upon Bob-
by Marion Francis’ Petition for Writ of
Habeas Corpus, filed on October 3, 1988.
Because the State had scheduled Francis’
execution for 7:00 A.M. on October 4, 1988,
this court granted an emergency motion
for stay of execution pending the resolu-
tion of the petition. For the reasons set
forth below, the Petition for Writ of Habe-
as Corpus is Denied.

Background

On March 29, 1985, a Dade County jury
convicted Francis of first-degree murder
for the 1975 death of Titus Walters.' The
jury recommended a life sentence, but the
trial judge overrode that recommendation
and imposed the death penalty.’ The ver-
dict and sentence were affirmed on direct
appeal. Francis v. State, 473 So.2d 672
(Fla.1985), cert. denied, 474 U.S. 1094, 106
S.Ct. 870, 88 L.Ed.2d 908 (1986). After the
Governor signed a death warrant for Fran-
cis on September 15, 1987, Francis filed
two collateral attacks on his death sen-
tence: a petition for writ of habeas corpus
in the Florida Supreme Court, and a Rule

3.850. motion, in, foe tia! soud 109? eta:
1987) (denying habeas petition) and Fran-
cis v. State, 529 So.2d 670 (Fla.1988) (af-
firming trial court’s denial of post-convic-

tion relief)? On September 8, 1988 the
1. The 1985 conviction was Francis’ third for this
crime. Two prior convictions by Monroe Coun-
ty juries had been reversed on direct appeal to
the Florida Supreme Court. For a full recita-

tion of the facts of this case, see Franci:
473 So.2d 672 (Fla.1985). are

2. ag Florida capital punishment statute pro-
vides that the jury’s sentence is advisory, and

may be overridden by the jud,
ei isis eo y the judge. See Fla.Stat.

3. The trial jud e also id
Phin g presided over the Rule

Florida Supreme Court denied Francis’ peti-
tion for rehearing on the 3.850 (Fla.R.
Crim.P.) Motion; the governor signed Fran-
cis’ second death warrant on September 12.
On October 3, Francis filed this habeas
petition. This court heard oral argument
on the petition on October 3 and again on
October 5.

The petition raises eight challenges to
Francis’ conviction and death sentence, and
requests an evidentiary hearing on most of
those claims. As the United States Court
of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit has
noted,

Case law clearly establishes that where
the facts are in dispute, the federal court
in habeas corpus must hold an evidentia-
ry hearing if the habeas applicant did not
receive a full and fair evidentiary hear-
ing in a state court.

Agan v. Dugger, 835 F.2d 1337, 1838 (11th
Cir.1987) (quoting Townsend v. Sain, 372
U.S. 293, 312-18, 83 S.Ct. 745, 756-57, 9
L.Ed.2d 770 (1963)). See also 28 U.S.C.
§ 2254 (1982). Aftera careful and detailed
review of the entire record in this case, and
after consideration of oral arguments
presented, the court concludes that each of
Francis’ challenges either raises no dis-
puted issues of fact, or was the subject of a
full and fair evidentiary hearing in state
court. Specifically, the court finds that the
alleged trial errors do not involve disputed
facts, and that the claim of ineffective as-
sistance of counsel was given full consider-

ation in the 285 beERRORS

of Deborah Wesley

IIA of this opinion, the court finds ample evi-
dence in the record, as did the Florida Supreme
Court, that the trial judge acted on “reasoned
judgment,” not out of retaliation for Francis’
refusal to accept a plea to a life sentence. See
Francis v. State, 473 So.2d at 677.

Defense Attorney Thomas Cassel-
man cross-examined the witness by
asking if he wasn’t present only be-
cause he had immunity.

“No, that is not the ease. If I had
gotten charged I would have been
here anyway. If I lie, I get charged,”
the witness said.

As the Colclasure murder trial en-
tered its 10th day in the Delta Coun-
ty Circuit Courtroom, the defense at-
torney called Joyce Cousins to take
the witness stand and testify before a
crowded courtroom. She appeared
composed and spoke in a well
modulated voice. .

She told the court she did not hire
anyone to kill her husband, did not
make false receipts showing pay-
ments had been made on a $15,000
loan, or enter into a murder for hire
agreement with Leon Christian.

- “It just doesn’t make any sense,”
she testified. “I loved my husband
and my children.”

She admitted that they had mar-
riage problems, that in the late
1970’s her husband abused her when
he was drunk, and, on one occasion,
attempted to choke her. However, the
children intervened and the attempt
failed. When Colclasure recognized
his problem and took treatments, he
stopped drinking, and her life
changed dramatically.

Joyce Cousins told the court it took
some time before she could fully
trust Colclasure not to begin drink-
ing again. He handled the finances

and gave her a weekly allowance of
$5, which she resented. Cousins had
to turn over her pay check to him.
However, he did give her extra mon-
ey when she needed it.

Cousins also testified that the first
time she became aware that Leon
Christian had killed her husband was
back in -1987, when Detective
Sergeant Leon Wildey approached
her and gave her the news.

The Defense Attorney centered his
attack on Clarence Christian, who
was originally charged with conspir-
acy to commit first degree murder.
Christian pleaded guilty to a lesser
charge of compounding a felony in
exchange for his testimony and a six-
month sentence in the county jail.
Casselman said that Clarence Chris-
tian admitted that he lied to the in-
vestigating authorities.

At the conclusion of the Defense
Attorney’s closing statement, Judge
Shipman instructed the jurors, after
which they retired to their chamber
to consider the evidence and testi-
mony presented to them during the
trial.

The jury deliberated for approxi-
mately 45 minutes on Friday night
and returned to resume their delib-
eration at 8 a.m. Saturday. At 2:50
p.m. they reached a verdict of guilty

LUCK RAN OUT

of conspiracy to commit first-degree
murder.

Colclasure’s family members and
friends burst into tears when the ver-
dict was read. Cousins bowed her
head weeping. A sister of the slain
man from Ontonagon, Michigan said,
“We can finally put it to rest. It’s like
a weight off your shoulders.”

Before being escorted out of the
courtroom, family members hugged
Joyce Cousins, as did her friends still
sobbing at the verdict.

Appearing before Circuit Court
Judge Dean Shipman in August
1992, in the Delta County Court-
room, Joyce Cousins was sentenced
to life in prison without parole. She
displayed no signs of emotion when
the sentence was pronounced.

Joyce Cousins, who has been held
in the Delta County Jail since her
conviction, will be assigned to the
Huron Valley Women’s Facility, in
Ypsilanti, to serve out her sentence.
This institution is the only prison for
women in the State of Michigan. *

Samuelson, Michelle Owens, Cindy John-
son, and Larry Holmes are not the real
names of the people involved in this story.
There can be no public interest in using
their real names.)

Confrontation clause does not prevent

Precluding cross-examination of wit-
ness about second-degree murder charge
against her violated confrontation clause;
trial judge prohibited inquiry into possibili-
ty that witness’ motivation was to prevent
increased charge to first-degree murder or
to obtain reduction to lesser charge or that
witness might be motivated to favor State.
U.S.C.A. Const.Amend. 6.

5. Criminal Law 662.7

Criminal defendant properly demon-
strates violation of confrontation clause by
showing that defendant was prevented
from conducting cross-examination that
would have exposed jury to facts from
which jurors could appropriately draw in-
ferences relating to reliability of witness.
U.S.C.A. Const.Amend. 6.

6. Criminal Law ¢1168(2)

Confrontation clause violation, as re-
sult of prohibition against cross-examina-
tion of witness about second-degree mur-
der charge against her, was harmless in
murder prosecution; all eyewitnesses testi-
fied as to same facts without any substan-
tial deviation; two disinterested witnesses
and physical evidence corroborated wit-
ness’ testimony; and witness was cross-ex-
amined extensively on many other areas.
U.S.C.A. Const.Amend. 6.

7. Criminal Law ¢=700(4)

Due process error, as result of State’s
failure to disclose agreement to allow for-
mer codefendant to plead guilty to third-de-
gree murder in exchange for testifying
truthfully at petitioner’s third trial, was
harmless in capital murder prosecution; co-
defendant acknowledged that she agreed to
testify in return for new trial or plea to

lesser offense and testified that she was
scheduled for resentencing and could get
pardoned; and record portrayed codefend-
ant as testifying out of her own self-inter-
est. U.S.C.A. Const.Amends. 5, 14.

Trial court could override jury’s recom-

Counsel’s failure to conduct more thor-

Petitioner sought habeas corpus relief

Nesbitt, J., held that: (1) confrontation

1. Criminal Law 662.4

When defendant’s guilt or innocence
may turn on reliability of witness, prosecu-
tion’s nondisclosure of evidence affecting
credibility or bias of witness may deprive
accused of guarantees of confrontation
clause. U.S.C.A. Const.Amend. 6.

2. Criminal Law ©662.7

Confrontation clause protects opportu-
nity for effective cross-examination, not

v. Giarratano, 622 F.2d 153, 156 n. 4 (5th Cir.
1980), the Court stated unequivocally that the
defendant must precisely define those factual
issues that were necessarily determined in the
prior trial.
