(continued on next page)

On April 27, 1978, Circuit Judge
Richard Fuller rejected the jury recom-
mendation because of the seriousness of
the crime and because of the defendant’s
prior. criminal record. An escapee from a
work-furlough program at the time of the
massacre, White, a drug user, had been
convicted in 1964 of aggravated rape and
had also received convictions on
narcotics-related charges.

Following a five-day trial in April, a
Dade jury convicted Marvin Francois,
32, of six first-degree murders, two at-
tempted murders, and three counts of
robbery. On April 24, the same jury re-
commended death and Judge Fuller
promptly sentenced him to the electric
chair.

Meanwhile, search continued for
30-year-old Ferguson, listed by the FBI
for unauthorized flight. Acting on an
informant’s tip, shortly after 7 a.m.,
Wednesday, April 5, 1978, Federal Ag-
ents Herbert Collins and Joseph E. Brun-
ter broke down the door of a first-floor
apartment in the Westview complex at
12601 NW 27th Ave., guns drawn. Fergu-
son, also armed, dropped his weapon.

In spite of intensive door-to-door
police searches of the neighborhood with
amugshot, Ferguson had been living
there under his own name, apparently
uinoticed, since shortly after the mas-
acre.

The loaded gun Ferguson surrendered
‘othe G-men, a .357 Ruger revolver, was
the same weapon used in the slaying of
wo Christian Club teenagers on Jan. 8,

1978, police discovered, when ballistics
tests were run. Ferguson is also a suspect
in several other crimes.

The two youngsters, Brian Glenfeldt
and Belinda Worley, both 17, and Hialeah
High School seniors, had gone for ice
cream, promising to be home early. Their
bodies were found the following day in a
trash-strewn field off LeJeune Road and
NW 128th Street. The girl had been
raped.

PSD Information Officer Lonnie
Lawrence told reporters that Ferguson
may be linked to a total of 11 homicides.
Miami Police, as noted, had already con-
nected him, also through ballistics, to the
Gold Dust Motel murders. PSD homicide
detectives identified him as a suspect in
the 1974 slaying of Joseph Walters whose
body was spotted near the railroad tracks
at NW 116th Court and 12th Street by a
passenger on a train.

In October of 1977, another teenage
couple, parked in the same desolate sec-
tion where the Hialeah youngster were

be BHU ies? 3

later killed, were accosted by a gunman.
When they refused to open the windows,
he fired through the glass, wounding
both, then fled. At least three women
have been assaulted by a rapist recently
in the same area. In November, 1977, an
armed man kidnapped a 17-year-old girl
from the Nova Machinery Co. inthe same
neighborhood. After several ransom de-
mands to Opa-Locka police, he released
the girl in a nearby field 12 hours later.
PSD detectives suspect the same man
committed all these crimes.

On Thursday, May 24, 1978, John
Errol Ferguson was convicted and sen-
tenced to death in the electric chair. In
order to separate each murder case for
the purposes of appeal, Judge Fuller pro-
nounced six consecutive death sen-
tences, plus two consecutive 30-year
terms for attempted murder, and two life
sentences for robbery.

tomatically subject to state Supreme

About the Sculptor,
Arnold Bergier

Long regarded as one of Amer-
ica’s most eminent sculptors,
Arnold Bergier was especially
commissioned to create this
Official Elvis Presiey Memorial
Belt Buckle. Mr. Bergier has
sculptured the Thomas Jeffer-
son Commemorative Medal and
many luminaries, including
Albert Einstein, Admirals William
Halsey and Chester Nimitz,
famed conductor Arturo Tosca-
nini, and renowned philosopher
John Dewey. Many of his works,
which also include coins and
medals, have been exhibited in
Prominent museums and institu-
tions around the world. With this
unique sculptured belt buckle.
truly he has created an heirloom
to treasure now, and to pass on
to future generations.

St. Valentine’s
Massacre:
Part 2.

(continued from page 53)

Gilbert Williams, 37. Not long after mid-
night Derringer had five names of the six
dead and assigned Dets. James McHugh
and Peter Caroddo to make a records
check. The wounded man taken to the
hospital was Johnny Hall, 44.

Neither Holmes nor Williams had prior
records. Stocker showed only traffic vio-
lations. Clayton, first identified by a de-
scription of his clothes which Sgt. Tom
Lawley obtained from the victim's
mother and wife, who were wailing out-
side, had been up on charges of carrying a
concealed firearm, possession of narco-
tics, burglary, and using a counterfeit
driver's license. Miller's rap sheet
showed burglary, receiving and conceal-
ing stolen property.

Det. Richard Mueller, canvassing the
neighborhood, reported back that an el-
derly couple about a block away had told
him of a suspicious visitor that night. A
black male, they said, had knocked about
8 p.m.., identifying himself as a technician
checking short circuits. The witnesses
had refused admittance and the man had
been picked up in an old blue station-
wagon which contained two or three
other black men. The witnesses de-
scribed the man as tall and thin with a
moderate Afro, wearing light blue pants
and a print shirt.

This description and account tallied
with the one obtained from the injured
Margaret Wooden at Parkway Hospital.
Her head wound had proved superficial
and she had given a formal statement.
Police had less luck with the hospitalized
man whose serious wound scheduled him
for brain surgery.

The descriptions of Margaret Wooden
and the neighbors were sufficient for a
composite. Margaret had also described a
second assailant as short, dark-skinned
and of heavy build.

The getaway T-bird was traced to the
Glenn Rental agency, as one neighbor.
hearing the gunfire, had been sufficiently
alert to get the tag number. Det. Steve
McElveen was dispatched to the agency.

Henry Clayton's wife told Sgt. Lawley
that her husband received a phone call
from Stocker about 9 p.m., requesting
him to take an unidentified man down-
town. Stocker had called from a market
described as a favorite hangout and repu-
ted rendezvous for drug exchanges @!

(continued on next page)

other illicit deals. According to his wife,
Stocker asked Clayton, ‘‘Are your pock-
ets full? I have three buyers.’’ This meant
he wanted to make a drug purchase. The
wife said it was for a Milwaukee buyer
who had obtained some cocaine earlier in
the day but wanted four ounces more.

Clayton had driven off in his old blue-
and-white Cadillac. When he was late re-
turning, she walked to Stocker’s, saw her
husband's car in front and police vehicles
all around. Neither she nor any of the
neighbors admitted knowing any
“Lucky.”

The other vehicle parked in front of
Stocker’s bore Wisconsin tags. Eventu-
ally, Williams’ common-law wife iden-
tified it as belonging to Caesar Hawkins,
aka Caesar Stinson, the out-of-state vis-
itor who had contacted Williams about
trying to purchase cocaine. He had been
staying at the Holiday Inn on 12th Ave.
and NW II Street. Det. McElveen
checked out his room without turning up
any useful leads.

In all, Counahan and his men lifted 505
latents from the murder house, took 130
photographs, collected approximately 15
shotgun pellets from beneath the head of
one victim, enmeshed in hair and brain
matter. They picked up miscellaneous
clothing that had been rummaged from
the drawers and closets and stewn about
the floor. Four or five marijuana cigaret-
tes were on top of the dresser in the mas-
ter bedroom and another cigarette and a
small brown envelope of pot were taken
from the living room. There were miscel-
laneous drugs in the bathroom cabinets,
but nothing overly suspicious.

Nevertheless, from the bits and pieces
collected that first night, all indications
pointed to drug-connected motivations
for the massacre. It was the kind of case
where successful solution might depend
much on informants and the intricate
Weaving together of many stray bits of
information. It required conferences and
cooperation from the narcotics squad and
the organized crime unit, many inter-
views with jail inmates, those being ar-
tested, and hundreds on the streets.

One step was to background the vic-
tims. The clear lead here was to the mar-
ket where, apparently, the victims had
been gathered before heading for
arabe home and a bloody rain of bul-
ets.

The proprietor there, shown polaroid
Photos of the victims, readily identified
them. He said Stocker had been at his
establishment about 9 p,m. the night of
the murder with Johnny Hall, PeeWee
Holmes, Gilbert Williams and a man from
out of town. He had heard Stocker make
the phone call to Henry Clayton. All of
them had left together.

Hall, when he was able to talk, con-
firmed this. He said it was dark when they
arrived at Stocker’s, and as there was an
old Cadillac in the driveway, Stocker

s

parked in front. A man was wiping the
Cadillac and joined them as they came up
the walk. As they entered the door, three
masked men carrying weapons came
from the back part of the house, the tall
**popeyed”’ one in front, the other two a
step behind on each side.

**l asked Stocker if this was what he
brought me to his house for,"’ said Hall in
his statement. “‘I told them, if you want
money, I can tell where there is plenty —
banks, loan companies...

MF vahisrgssij

“They made us get down. I heard a
lady crying in the bedroom... Another
knock came on the door. They got that
one and tied him with his hands behind...
They went through our pockets, the tall
one... Stocker was crying. He told them
where the shotgun was and shells, and a
.38 in the drawer... | was dragged into the
bedroom by my belt.

St. Valentine’s Massacre: Part 2

(continued from page 55)

head. Down the line they went. Boom!
Boom! Boom! Shot them all in the back of
the head. except me. After they shot
Stocker, they walked around me.”

The men had argued about the killing,
Hall said. One wanted to leave after col-
lecting the money, rings, and $150 watch.
But one man’s mask had slipped, and he
insisted. **We got to kill all these people.”

‘They shot me last,"’ Hall's statement
continued. **l turned my head to the side
and played dead..."

Hall, an admitted drug user, also said
he had seen the men before around [Sth
Avenue and 7Ist Street, a neighborhood
of brisk traffic in narcotics.

The T-bird was found on NW 65th
Street. The Crime Lab found all the
smooth surfaces usually dusted for fing-
erprints had been heavily smeared with
grease.

About a week and a half after the mas-
sacre, ballistics discovered that a projec-

tile removed by the medical examiner
from one of the Carol City victims was
shot from the same gun as the fatal bullets
in acase under investigation by the Miami
Police Dept. Miami Det. Robert Gayle
briefed PSD detectives and was assigned
also to aid in the Carol City investigation.

On May 31, an elderly couple from St.
Petersburg, in Miami for a relative’s fun-
eral, had been found bound and shot to
death in their room at the Gold Dust
Motel. A brown wicker purse belonging
to 82-year-old Mrs. Katherine Berry, had
been discovered floating in the Little
River Canal which runs by the motel. In-
side was Mrs. Berry's identification anda
post card advertising the Gold Dust Motel
at 7700 Biscayne Boulevard.

The finder had notified the hotel man-
ager who, unable to rouse her guests by
telephone, opened the door with a pass-
key, found the bodies, and called police.
75-year-old Raymond Berry's wallet was

empty except for his identification and
the couple's 1975 Ford Gran Torino was
missing. Robbery appeared to have been
the motive. Police had no hard leads until
the ballistics match linked the Gold Dust
murders to the Carol City massacre.

The collaboration between the two
police departments was helpful. Miami
-had background information on Clayton
and Stocker and their involvement in nar-
cotics. Stocker was heavy into drugs on
the street level. Clayton was a source.
Several people large in narcotics were
dealing with them.

A major break came on August 26. An
inmate in the Dade County Jail, awaiting
trialona robbery charge, hoped to curry a
favorable deal by offering information in
the Carol City case. He told Det. Der-
ringer he had read in the newspapers that
some of the murders had been committed
with a shotgun. He knew a street charac-
ter heavy in narcotics who kept a shotgun
in a briefcase and had it with him the
afternoon of the massacre.

The man, much disliked, distrusted,
and feared on the street was Marvin
Francois, better known as **Robbin’
Marvin"* because of frequent rip-offs in
drug deals. On July 27, the informant had
seen Marvin leave a ‘‘shooting gallery”
or dope house on 15th Ave. and 69th
Street with a man called Adolphus and a
man he did not recognize. Marvin had his
briefcase.

The robber had a small deal going with
Marvin and was anxious because of the
latter’s reputation for robbing dope
pushers. Adolphus reassured him.
“Don’t worry about Marvin. He's got
bigger things on today.”

The three men drove off in an old Ram-
bler stationwagon. Police were alieady
hunting such a vehicle. It had been de-
scribed by the elderly couple as picking
up the phoney ‘wire checker’ after they
refused to admit him.

A couple of days later a junkie picked
up by police told detectives he was a
friend of Marvin Francois and identified
Adolphus as Adolphus Archie. He, too.
had seen them around the [Sth Avenue
‘dope house”’ the afternoon on July 27.
They had been with John Ferguson.
which the junkie said was very unusual.
He had never seen Ferguson in that
neighborhood before, and furthermore.
was surprised to see him with Robbin
Marvin. It was well known that Ferguson
did not use dope and even tended to
**preach”’ against its use.

The junkie had also seen Marvin just
the day before, on 13th Avenue around
62nd Street. Francois wanted dope, bul
he wasn’t going to pay for it. When the
pusher was slow to hand it over, Marvin
said, *‘Don’t be like them guys in Carol
City.”

(continued on next page)

Ferguson was on probation. Shown his
mugshots, both Margaret and the elderly
couple identified him as the fake **wire”’
man. Archie’s stationwagon was found in
a Bunche Park junk car yard at the north
end of town. It was not drivable and was
towed in for processing. Tech. Police
lifted 19 latents, but none were of value.

On August 31, detectives picked up
Archie at 20731 NW 37 Ave. and brought
him in for questioning. They read him his
rights on the way. Even though grilled by
Derringer and Det. Fabio Alonzo. Archie
was persistent in his denials of complic-
ity. He did, however, agree to take a
polygraph test and an appointment was
scheduled for the next evening with a
Miami expert, Edward L. DuBois III.

Archie did so badly on the first try that
DuBois told him he had flunked and urged
him to cooperate. “‘His face began to
shake and his lips quivered,’* DuBois tes-
tified later, stating that Archie broke
down. ‘I didn’t kill anybody! But | know
all about it.” *

Archie talked to DuBois for about an
hour and when the expert came out and
informed Derringer and Det. Diego
Mella, Archie was arrested there in
DuBois’ office and signed a rights waiver.
He was taken to the station for a formal
statement, completed at 4:15 a.m.

In his statement, Adolphus Archie told
police that one of his sidelines was as a
driver on shoplifting expeditions. He said
he had driven Marvin about three months

earlier but quit when they quarreled
about money.

On July 27, Archie ran into John Fergu-
son who he hadn’t seen for about four
years. The two men went into a local
dope house even though Ferguson was
against drugs. About 15 minutes later,
Marvin entered. The two tried to con-
vince Archie to drive them to Carol City
and as they talked, a man named Bue-
ford White entered the place.

Archie continued with his statement,
saying that he finally agreed to drive the
men for $200 and that they met later at

White's motel room. There, Marvin and
Ferguson checked out their guns and
Marvin, Archie and White shot some
dope.

When the four men arrived in Carol
City, they dropped Ferguson off on a
corner and watched as he approached the
door of a single family house. It was al-
most dark now and after about 15 mi-
nutes, Marvin and White got out.

“TL started to pull away.”’ Archie's
statement continued. **But Marvin said,

of the State of Florida, for Alachua County, at the Spring

_ The Grana Jurors of the State of Florida, enquiring in
and for the body of the County of Alachua upon their oaths
present that Fortune Ferguson, Jr., alias Jew Ferguson late

‘of the County of Alachua aforesaid, in the Circuit and
State aforesaid, laborer, on the 4th day of June, in the
year of our Lord, one thousand nine hundred and twenty-four,
with force of arms and in the County of Alachua aforesaid,
in and upon one Louise Patterson, a female ohild under the
age of ten years, to-wit: of the age of eight years, un=
lawfally and feloniously did make an assault, and she, the

then and ‘there forcibly and felon-
iously did carnally know and abuse.

Against the form of the Statute in such case made and
to the evil example of, all others in the like’
ase offending, and against the peace and dignity of the
State of Florida.

J. CG. Adkins,
State's Attorney for the‘ Eighth Judicial Cirouit
of the State of Florida, Prosecuting in the

STARKE, Fla., May 27 (UPI) — A

Mass-killer
Francois
executed

STARKE, Fla. (AP) — Marvin Fran-
cois, who shotgunned six people to
death execution style because his mask
fell off and exposed his face while he
was robbing a “drug house,” was elec-
trocuted in Florida’s electric chair
today.

Francois was put to death shortly
after 7 a.m. at the Florida State Prison.

It was the third execution in Florida
this year, the 13th since the state rein-
Stated the death penalty in 1976 and the
44th in the country since capital pun-
ishment was reinstated the same year.

Francois, 39, was convicted in the
July 27, 1977, execution-style killings of
six people during the robbery of what
police termed a “drug house” in the
Miami suburb of Carol City.

The U.S. Supreme Court late Tues-
day denied a plea by Francois to post-
pone the execution.

The 11th U.S. Circuit Court of
Appeals denied Francois’ motions for a
Stay earlier Tuesday. He was scheduled

~ to have been executed Tuesday morn-

sing but won a 24-hour stay from the
~“11th Circuit when the appeal was filed

~*there.

AWAKENED AT 4:30 a.m. today,
Francois was served a breakfast of lob-
ster, shrimp, ribs, chicken and french
fries. “He ate all of it but a little bit of
the ribs,” said Vernon Bradford,
Department of Corrections spokesman.

Francois’ last visitors, who left at 1
a.m., were his mother Muriel Hollings-
worth of Miami, his girlfriend Juanita
Pace, and his teenage twins, Alexis and
Aleasian Rolle, Bradford said.

Francois and two other men, all
armed and masked, entered a home to
take drugs or money from the occu-

pants, police said.

Assistant State Attorney David
Waksman said at the time of Francois’
trial that he went to the home to kill a

sane

Francois

man thought to be muscling in on the
drug business. ;

The three tied up the two occupants
before the owner of the house returned
with five others. They also were bound.

after Francois’ mask fell off, he said all
eight would have to be killed. Francois
shot six of the victims in the head with
a shotgun in the living room. Two
others were wounded but survived and
testified against Francois, whose
accomplices also were sentenced to
die.

During a 1982 clemency interview,
Francois said he never knew his father
well and saw little of his mother
because she worked as a maid. He tes-
tified that he tried heroin at age 16 and
he maintained a $300-$400 a day habit
in 1976.

He was characterized in the clem-
ency hearing by his public defender,
Rory Stein of Miami, as a poor youth
raised without supervision in a wooden

. Shack and on the streets, where as a

“weak man” he turned to heroin addic-
tion.

Two other Florida inmates are
scheduled to die next Tuesday. They
are Ian Lightbourn, 25, sentenced to

Francois, who shotgunned six
people to death execution style
because his mask fell off and ex-

tric chair.

chamber at the Florida State
Prison and was strapped into the
chair at 7 a.m. He was pro-
nounced dead at 7:18, said Sgt.

Don Gladish of the Department of

Corrections.

read to witnesses. ‘‘The black
race has lost its pride and dignity
and is slowly dying from within
and without. My death ends my

tears, and the fortune of watching
my race slowly die.’’

Francois added, “If there is such

a thing as an anti-Christ, it ain’t
one man but the whole white
race.”’

VOL. 73, NO. 148

Florida kills Viet Nam vet in electric chair

BY MONI BASU

FLAMBEAU EDITOR DESIGNATE

David Livingston Funchess, a decorated Viet Nam war
veteran, died in Florida’s electric chair Tuesday afternoon after
Gov. Bob Graham refused him executive clemency and the
U.S. Supreme Court denied him a stay of execution.

Funchess had originally been scheduled to die at 7 a.m.
Tuesday morning but a panel of the 11th Circuit Court of
Appeal in Atlanta granted him a five-hour stay so the High
Court would have time to rule on the case. The Supreme Court
delayed the execution another five hours but voted 7-2 to reject
the appeal. Following a. two-minute surge of 2,000 volts,
Funchess, 39, was pronounced dead at 5.11 p.m. He is the
15th man to die in the state’s electric chair since the death
penalty was reinstated in 1976 and the first Viet Nam veteran
to be executed in the nation.

When asked by prison officials if he wanted to make a last
statement to the press, Funchess said ‘‘No.’

Defense attorneys argued the ex-Marine suffered from: Post
Traumatic Stress Disorder—a recently recognized war-induced
mental illness—at the time he was convicted for two 1974
Jacksonville bar murders. In their court appeals and request
for excutive clemency from the governor, they said PTSD was
never méntioned during Funchess’ 1975 trial or sentencing.

‘‘This man came back from Viet Nam in real bad shape,”’
said Tom Fischer, a member of Veterans for Peace who spent
one year in Viet Nam. ‘“That was never considered in court.
We're protestingthe fact that he was executed without
considering that. a0 ignore it is to ignore him as a human

thers gathered for a second time at the Viet
Nam Veterans Memorial across from the Old Capitol on
Monroe Street for a vigil following the execution Tuesday
afternoon. The group had protested the execution earlier at
a noon vigil. Fischer told reporters that if the governor or other

politicians who were present at the dedication of the war

‘monument last November had respect for those who fought
_in Viet Nam, they wouid have reconsidered Funchess’ case.

__.. “Not long ago, when this (the memorial) was Suilt, Gov. .
2 Graham and other politicians stood here and said that it was .
‘time to separate the warriors from the war,’’ said Fischer, —

ee ee re same Mamniean

i gFunchess’ ee,

One more name

Opponents of the death penalty, veterans and
supporters of David Funchess joined the vigil at the
Viet Nam War Memoria! at noon Tuesday to protest
the ex-Marine’s execution. Photo by Deborah Thomas

Graham a friend of Viet Nam vets.”’

Despite appeals from defense attorneys, Graham refused
executive clemency to Funchess Monday. ‘The governor’s legal
advisor Art Weidinger said the effects of PTSD on the former
soidier had already been presented to Graham at his first
ciemency hearing im 1982.

“He (Graham) didn’t. believe PTSD was a factor in

considering’ clemency,” Weidinger said Tuesday. He said
Graham feels Funchess’ case has been litigated fully in the
courts.

But Michael Mello, a lawyer for Capital Collateral
Review—a state-funded agency that represents indigent Death
Row inmates—said the issue at hand was that PTSD had not
been presented as mitigating evidence to the jury that tried
Funchess for murder in 1975. He said PTSD had not even been
recognized as a genuine illness back then, but regardless of
that, Funchess’ trial lawyers should have included ‘his 1967
tour of Viet Nam as part of the evidence. ‘‘That’s where
it (the evidence) counted,’’ said Mello. ‘‘Once you’ve already
been convicted, there’s a real inertia to commute the death
sentence to life in prison. David’s trial lawyer could have done
more—the jury could have been told he was a decorated war
hero, they could have been told about his childhood. All of
that would’ve been incredible mitigating evidence to the jury
even though PTSD had not been diagnosed,’’ Mello said.

According to court affadavits, Funchess never committed
a crime before going to Viet Nam. But he returned from
Southeast Asia a drastically changed man.

At the late afternoon vigil, people looked at their watches.
It was 5:20. ‘‘It must be over by now,’’ one woman told
another. Others Neld each other and wept. Still others stared
‘at the color photograph of Funchess in his Marine uniform
placed atop a basket of flowers. The group formed a circle
in between the two huge granite columns that form the war
monument.

Rev. Jim Hardison, coordinator of the death penalty project
for Florida IMPACT—an interfaith lobby group for social
justice issues—said he was angered not by capital punishment
per sé but by the way the state administers it.

_ “Again we’ve taken a poor, penniless, minority person who
was mentally ill and executed him,’’ Hardison said.

Others present said they felt compelled to speak.

‘‘We’re really appalled by your callous indifference toward
David Funchess,” said Linda Reynolds, Director of the Florida
Clearinghouse on Criminal. Justice, referring to the governor.
‘*Viet Nam veterans will not forget what you’ve done today.

- “David Funchess was killed twice by society,” Reynolds
said. ‘‘Once in Viet Nam and once today.”’

Managing Editor

Misuse of death penalty

XECUTION is the ultimate penalty
for the ultimate crime, the deliberate
taking of a life. To bar the death pen-

alty entirely is to ensure in some cases the
denial of an essential principle of justice:
that the punishment fit the crime.

Yet this ultimate penalty must be used
with great care. It is warranted only when
the crime was particularly heinous and the
legal processes were above reproach.

That is why Gov. Lawton Chiles should
stay today’s scheduled execution of Bobby
Francis, a Miamian convicted in the 1975
torture killing of a Key West drug infor-
mant. Although his crime fits most defini-
tions of heinous, the processes that brought
Francis to Death Row are suspect.

Leave aside the murky circumstances of
the killing itself — the actions of the mur-
der victim, the involvement of other per-
sons who may have incriminated Francis to
save themselves from harsher penalties.

Also leave aside that Francis twice was
condemned in Key West in flawed trials set
aside on appeal. At the third trial, in Miami,
a jury found him guilty but also recom-
mended a life sentence. The judge overrode
the jury and sentenced Francis to die.

That’s the real issue here. Of the 37
states with the death penalty, only three —
Florida, Alabama, and Indiana — let judges

Pe encunuaimnmaipegerenrtl
WHEN JUDGE OVERRIDES JURY

override juries. Moreover, since 1986 the
Florida Supreme Court has overturned the
death penalty in 37 of the 42 cases in which
a judge overrode the jury's recommenda-
tion of life. Francis’s appeal was heard a
year earlier, in 1985. :

In a report to Governor Chiles earlier
this year, the Criminal Justice Task Force
recommended changing Florida law “‘so
that a judge could not override a jury’s rec-
ommendation of life imprisonment.”

The Legislature ought to do so. As The
Herald noted in 1985: “To permit one
judge to defy the community’s conscience
— and that’s what a jury’s recommendation
represents — is thus to subvert the public
consensus upon which the law is founded.”

For the state of Florida to execute Fran-
cis on the eve of a possible change in the law
would amount to inflicting a punishment
that is now most unusual, if not excessively
cruel given the circumstances of this case.

Barring last-minute Federal interven-
tion, only a gubernatorial stay can buy the
time to argue the override issue as it affects
Francis’s hopes for clemency. The gover-
nor should grant that precious gift of time.

Sun staff and wire report

STARKE — Blasting the white race as the “antl-
Christ” killers of fellow-plack “scapegoats,” mass
murderer Marvin Francois was electrocuted
Wednesday morning for the 1977 execution-style
killings of six Miami area residents.

“If there is such a thing as the anti-Christ, itis not “
‘my death ends my tears and years of torture of

one man, but the whole white race,” Francois, 39,
said moments after being strapped tightly into the
oak electric chair at Florida State Prison.

In the longest statement yet delivered by a con-
demned man, Francois read his handwritten blast’
at Dade County officials.

“Miami's white racist judiciary and law-enforce-
ment systems have made me, the once heroin ad-”

dict, a scapegoat with fabricated assertions,
dishonest policemen, unethical judges, unethical
state attorneys, drug pushers, drug users for their
political and personal ambitions and their unwill-
ingness to control the drug sphere of Miami
communities.

“The black race has lost its pride and dignity...

watching my race slowly die,” said Francois, a
former heroin addict with an arrest record dating
back to 1966,

He jerked back when the voltage struck: his
hands clenched. Ten minutes passed before Fran-
cois was declared dead, about four minutes longer
than in past electrocutions. $

~

“Your heart is a strong muscle. It takes a while
to shut. down,” explained Lori Naslund of the
Gainesville medical examiner’s office, where an
autopsy was performed. “It did take a little longer
but I can’t say if it was because he was in such good
shape. It could be because of his physical
condition,” .

Judges from the state level to the U.S. Supreme
Court had denied last-ditch legal pleas to spare
Francois, one of three men sentenced to die for
murdering six people in a drug-related robbery in
July 1977,

All eight were shot in the head, but

convict Francois and his co-

Another accomplice, Adolphus Ar-

Carol City home. He pleaded guilty in

“Marvin kind of smiled and told it
like the St. Valentine’s massacre,” Ar-

Francois’ mother had testified in
her son's defense. “He was a good son
to me. He was good to everybody,”
Muriel Hollingsworth told the jury.

Mrs. Hollingsworth was among
Francois’ last visitors Tuesday night.
His girlfriend, Juanita Pace, and
teen-age twins Alexis and Aleasian
Rolle also visited with the hulking
Death Row inmate.

From 1 a.m. Wednesday until 4:30,
Francois was alone inside his death-
watch cell, a few steps from the exe-
cution chamber. At 4:30 a.m., prison
chefs prepared a last meal of lobster,
shrimp, ribs, fried chicken and
french fries for Francois.

“He ate all but a little bit of the
ribs,” said Department of Corrections

Afterward, Francois’ head was
shaved to improve electrical contact.
He walked into the death chamber at
7 a.m., and read his lengthy statement
from a sheet held by prison Superin-
tendent Richard Dugger.

A black mask was then lowered
over his face. At a nod from Dugger,
the black-hooded, anonymous execu-
tioner — who received $150 cash in
hand for the work — switched the cir-
cuit on, sending 2,000 volts into Fran-

cois. He was pronounced dead at 7:18
a.m.

“The heinous crimes committed by
Marvin Francois underscore the vio-
lence as well as the human tragedy
which flow into our state as a result of
drugs,” said Gov. Bob Graham, who
has signed 106 death warrants since
taking office in 1979. “I trust that the
strong action taken today will under-
score our determination to eradicate
this scourge.”

About 25 people, some holding
signs bearing slogans such as “Re-
spect Life, Stop Executions” and “No
More Vengeance, Stop the Death Pen-
alty,” gathered in front of the Capitol
at noon to protest Francois’
execution.

They read from the Bible and
prayed as one death-penalty support-
er stood by with a sign saying, “‘God’s
Law Commands It. The Governor's
Stand on Capital Punishment Is Just.”

Francois was the 13th Death Row
inmate executed in Florida since the
U.S. Supreme Court lifted its death-
penalty ban in 1976. He was the third
Florida inmate electrocuted this
year.

Two other Florida prisoners are
scheduled to die next Tuesday. They
are Ian Lightbourn, 25, sentenced to
death for the Jan. 16, 1981 rape-slay-
ing of Nancy A. O'Farrell of Marion
County, and Oscar Mason, 25, sen-
tenced for the March 19, 1980 stab-
bing death of Linda Sue Chapman in
Tampa.
